========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I came in because I just haven’t been feeling like myself lately. I’m a teacher, and the district put this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on my plate on top of my regular teaching responsibilities, and I know that probably sounds like just another school initiative, because there are always initiatives, but this one has really gotten under my skin.

I’m still showing up, I’m still getting through the school day, I’m not falling apart or anything, but I feel wound up almost all the time and discouraged in a way that’s not typical for me. And I’ve noticed I’m putting off lesson plans and emails—things I used to just sit down and handle without even thinking much about it. Now I’ll stare at them, or tell myself I’ll do them later, and then they just sit there. That’s really not how I usually operate, so I thought maybe I should talk to someone before it gets worse.

I wouldn’t say sad most of the time, exactly. Discouraged, yes. Wound up, definitely. It’s more like since the district added this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on top of my regular teaching, I’ve felt kind of unlike myself.

Yes, I do. I mean, I don’t know if I’d call it worrying in the sense of imagining disasters, exactly, but my mind keeps going back to it. I’ll be getting through the school day, and I am getting through it, but I feel wound up and discouraged in a way that really doesn’t feel like me. And then when I think about what’s coming up—lesson plans, emails, whatever needs to be done for this initiative—I sort of freeze or avoid it. Those are things I used to just sit down and handle without much thought, and now I find myself putting them off, which then gives me more to think about. So yes, it’s been taking up a lot more space in my head than I’d like.

Like if I’m doing something I usually like, part of my brain is still circling around school stuff or thinking about what I should be doing instead. So I wouldn’t say I can’t enjoy things, but I’m not sure I’m enjoying them in the same relaxed way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad most of the time since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say sad most of the time, exactly. Discouraged, yes. Wound up, definitely. It’s more like since the district added this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on top of my regular teaching, I’ve felt kind of unlike myself.\n\nI’m still getting through the day and doing my job, but there’s this constant pressure in the background, and I notice myself putting off lesson plans and emails that I used to just handle without making a whole production out of it. So it’s not that I’m crying all day or feeling deeply sad every minute. It’s more that I feel tense and worn down and kind of disheartened in a way that doesn’t feel normal for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about things that are coming up, like the school initiative or your teaching responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. I mean, I don’t know if I’d call it worrying in the sense of imagining disasters, exactly, but my mind keeps going back to it. Ever since the district put this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on me in addition to my regular teaching, it’s like there’s this constant hum in the background.\n\nI’ll be getting through the school day, and I am getting through it, but I feel wound up and discouraged in a way that really doesn’t feel like me. And then when I think about what’s coming up—lesson plans, emails, whatever needs to be done for this initiative—I sort of freeze or avoid it. Those are things I used to just sit down and handle without much thought, and now I find myself putting them off, which then gives me more to think about. So yes, it’s been taking up a lot more space in my head than I’d like.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious most of the time since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Most of the time might be a little strong, but… yes, I’ve felt much more wound up than usual. It’s not like I’m panicking all day or anything like that. I still show up, I still teach, I still do what I’m supposed to do during the day.\n\nBut ever since the district added this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on top of my regular teaching, I feel like I’m carrying this pressure around with me. I’m discouraged in a way that doesn’t really feel like me, and I notice I’m putting off things like lesson plans and emails—things I used to just sit down and do without turning them into this whole ordeal in my head.\n\nSo I don’t know if I’d say “anxious most of the time” exactly, but I do feel tense and preoccupied with it a lot of the time.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Ever since the district put this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on me in addition to my regular teaching, it’s like there’s this constant hum in the background.\n\nI’ll be getting through the school day, and I am getting through it, but I feel wound up and discouraged in a way that really doesn’t feel like me. And then when I think about what’s coming up—lesson plans, emails, whatever needs to be done for this initiative—I sort of freeze or avoid it. Those are things I used to just sit down and handle without much thought, and now I find myself putting them off, which then gives me more to think about. So yes, it’s been taking up a lot more space in my head than I’d like.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious most of the time since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Most of the time might be a little strong, but… yes, I’ve felt much more wound up than usual. It’s not like I’m panicking all day or anything like that. I still show up, I still teach, I still do what I’m supposed to do during the day.\n\nBut ever since the district added this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on top of my regular teaching, I feel like I’m carrying this pressure around with me. I’m discouraged in a way that doesn’t really feel like me, and I notice I’m putting off things like lesson plans and emails—things I used to just sit down and do without turning them into this whole ordeal in my head.\n\nSo I don’t know if I’d say “anxious most of the time” exactly, but I do feel tense and preoccupied with it a lot of the time.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in your weight since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Not that I’ve really noticed, no. I mean, my clothes seem to fit about the same, and I haven’t been weighing myself regularly enough to say, “Oh, it’s exactly the same” or anything like that.\n\nI suppose with everything going on I haven’t been paying close attention to it, but nothing has stood out to me as a real weight change.\n\n[Last Question]\nAre you avoiding certain situations or places because of how you've been feeling?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I wouldn’t say I’m avoiding places, exactly. I still go to school, I still stand in front of the class, I still get through the teaching day. It’s more that I’ve started avoiding certain tasks connected to all of this, if that makes sense.

Since the district put this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on my plate on top of my regular teaching, I notice I’ll put off sitting down with lesson plans or responding to emails related to it. Those are things I used to just do—maybe not joyfully, but without this big internal hurdle. Now I’ll find myself finding other things to do first, or telling myself I’ll get to it later, and then it’s hanging over me.

So no, not avoiding a building or a particular room or anything like that. It’s more avoiding the work that makes me feel wound up and discouraged.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think so, not in the sense of feeling numb or detached from myself. I still feel things pretty clearly—maybe too clearly sometimes, honestly. I know when I’m frustrated, or discouraged, or tense. I’m not walking around feeling blank or like I’m watching my life from the outside.

There are moments during the school day where I’m very much on autopilot, but that’s also kind of part of teaching sometimes, you know? You move from one thing to the next because the day demands it. But I wouldn’t say I feel emotionally disconnected. I’m present, I’m just… tired of having this particular pressure sitting there in the background.

Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure I’d say I no longer enjoy things, exactly. I still can enjoy things when I’m actually doing them, if that makes sense. Like if I’m with my family, or if I’m watching something I like, or doing something quiet at home, I can still feel that it’s pleasant.

I think what’s changed more is that it takes a little more effort to get myself into those things, because my mind is kind of pulled back toward school and what I still need to deal with. So sometimes something that should feel relaxing doesn’t feel as relaxing as it normally would, but I wouldn’t say the pleasure is gone.

It’s more like the stress is crowding the edges of things I normally enjoy, not that I can’t enjoy them at all.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because I just haven’t been feeling like myself lately. I’m a teacher, and the district put this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on my plate on top of my regular teaching responsibilities, and I know that probably sounds like just another school initiative, because there are always initiatives, but this one has really gotten under my skin.\n\nI’m still showing up, I’m still getting through the school day, I’m not falling apart or anything, but I feel wound up almost all the time and discouraged in a way that’s not typical for me. And I’ve noticed I’m putting off lesson plans and emails—things I used to just sit down and handle without even thinking much about it. Now I’ll stare at them, or tell myself I’ll do them later, and then they just sit there. That’s really not how I usually operate, so I thought maybe I should talk to someone before it gets worse.\n\nDoctor: Are you still enjoying activities or hobbies that you typically found pleasurable before feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that, honestly. I mean, I haven’t really sat down and taken inventory of that. I still do some of the things I normally do, or at least I intend to, but I don’t know if I’ve noticed a clear “I don’t enjoy anything anymore” kind of thing.\n\nIt’s more like everything has this layer of distraction on top of it, if that makes sense. Like if I’m doing something I usually like, part of my brain is still circling around school stuff or thinking about what I should be doing instead. So I wouldn’t say I can’t enjoy things, but I’m not sure I’m enjoying them in the same relaxed way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad most of the time since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say sad most of the time, exactly. Discouraged, yes. Wound up, definitely. It’s more like since the district added this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on top of my regular teaching, I’ve felt kind of unlike myself.\n\nI’m still getting through the day and doing my job, but there’s this constant pressure in the background, and I notice myself putting off lesson plans and emails that I used to just handle without making a whole production out of it. So it’s not that I’m crying all day or feeling deeply sad every minute. It’s more that I feel tense and worn down and kind of disheartened in a way that doesn’t feel normal for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about things that are coming up, like the school initiative or your teaching responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. I mean, I don’t know if I’d call it worrying in the sense of imagining disasters, exactly, but my mind keeps going back to it. Ever since the district put this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on me in addition to my regular teaching, it’s like there’s this constant hum in the background.\n\nI’ll be getting through the school day, and I am getting through it, but I feel wound up and discouraged in a way that really doesn’t feel like me. And then when I think about what’s coming up—lesson plans, emails, whatever needs to be done for this initiative—I sort of freeze or avoid it. Those are things I used to just sit down and handle without much thought, and now I find myself putting them off, which then gives me more to think about. So yes, it’s been taking up a lot more space in my head than I’d like.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious most of the time since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Most of the time might be a little strong, but… yes, I’ve felt much more wound up than usual. It’s not like I’m panicking all day or anything like that. I still show up, I still teach, I still do what I’m supposed to do during the day.\n\nBut ever since the district added this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on top of my regular teaching, I feel like I’m carrying this pressure around with me. I’m discouraged in a way that doesn’t really feel like me, and I notice I’m putting off things like lesson plans and emails—things I used to just sit down and do without turning them into this whole ordeal in my head.\n\nSo I don’t know if I’d say “anxious most of the time” exactly, but I do feel tense and preoccupied with it a lot of the time.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in your weight since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Not that I’ve really noticed, no. I mean, my clothes seem to fit about the same, and I haven’t been weighing myself regularly enough to say, “Oh, it’s exactly the same” or anything like that.\n\nI suppose with everything going on I haven’t been paying close attention to it, but nothing has stood out to me as a real weight change.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding certain situations or places because of how you've been feeling?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’m avoiding places, exactly. I still go to school, I still stand in front of the class, I still get through the teaching day. It’s more that I’ve started avoiding certain tasks connected to all of this, if that makes sense.\n\nSince the district put this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on my plate on top of my regular teaching, I notice I’ll put off sitting down with lesson plans or responding to emails related to it. Those are things I used to just do—maybe not joyfully, but without this big internal hurdle. Now I’ll find myself finding other things to do first, or telling myself I’ll get to it later, and then it’s hanging over me.\n\nSo no, not avoiding a building or a particular room or anything like that. It’s more avoiding the work that makes me feel wound up and discouraged.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt disconnected from your emotions or like you're just going through the motions of daily life without feeling much of anything?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in the sense of feeling numb or detached from myself. I still feel things pretty clearly—maybe too clearly sometimes, honestly. I know when I’m frustrated, or discouraged, or tense. I’m not walking around feeling blank or like I’m watching my life from the outside.\n\nThere are moments during the school day where I’m very much on autopilot, but that’s also kind of part of teaching sometimes, you know? You move from one thing to the next because the day demands it. But I wouldn’t say I feel emotionally disconnected. I’m present, I’m just… tired of having this particular pressure sitting there in the background.\n\nDoctor: Are there things you used to enjoy that you no longer find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I no longer enjoy things, exactly. I still can enjoy things when I’m actually doing them, if that makes sense. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, I do feel guilty about it, probably more than I’d like to admit. I’m still showing up, and I’m still teaching, and the kids are being taught—I don’t want to make it sound like I’ve stopped doing my job. The basic responsibilities are getting done.

But there are things around the planning and the rollout work that I’ve either missed the deadline for or pushed back longer than I should have. And that bothers me because I’m not usually that person. I’ve always been the one who has the materials ready, answers the email, turns things in when they’re due, keeps track of the moving pieces.

Lately I’ve had colleagues remind me about things I used to remember on my own, and even when they’re kind about it, I feel embarrassed. I’ll think, “Why didn’t I just do that already?” or “People are going to think I’m dropping the ball.” So yes, there’s definitely guilt there—not constant every second, but it hangs over me when I know I’ve let something slide.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because I just haven’t been feeling like myself lately. I’m a teacher, and the district put this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on my plate on top of my regular teaching responsibilities, and I know that probably sounds like just another school initiative, because there are always initiatives, but this one has really gotten under my skin.\n\nI’m still showing up, I’m still getting through the school day, I’m not falling apart or anything, but I feel wound up almost all the time and discouraged in a way that’s not typical for me. And I’ve noticed I’m putting off lesson plans and emails—things I used to just sit down and handle without even thinking much about it. Now I’ll stare at them, or tell myself I’ll do them later, and then they just sit there. That’s really not how I usually operate, so I thought maybe I should talk to someone before it gets worse.\n\nDoctor: Are you still enjoying activities or hobbies that you typically found pleasurable before feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that, honestly. I mean, I haven’t really sat down and taken inventory of that. I still do some of the things I normally do, or at least I intend to, but I don’t know if I’ve noticed a clear “I don’t enjoy anything anymore” kind of thing.\n\nIt’s more like everything has this layer of distraction on top of it, if that makes sense. Like if I’m doing something I usually like, part of my brain is still circling around school stuff or thinking about what I should be doing instead. So I wouldn’t say I can’t enjoy things, but I’m not sure I’m enjoying them in the same relaxed way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad most of the time since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say sad most of the time, exactly. Discouraged, yes. Wound up, definitely. It’s more like since the district added this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on top of my regular teaching, I’ve felt kind of unlike myself.\n\nI’m still getting through the day and doing my job, but there’s this constant pressure in the background, and I notice myself putting off lesson plans and emails that I used to just handle without making a whole production out of it. So it’s not that I’m crying all day or feeling deeply sad every minute. It’s more that I feel tense and worn down and kind of disheartened in a way that doesn’t feel normal for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about things that are coming up, like the school initiative or your teaching responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. I mean, I don’t know if I’d call it worrying in the sense of imagining disasters, exactly, but my mind keeps going back to it. Ever since the district put this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on me in addition to my regular teaching, it’s like there’s this constant hum in the background.\n\nI’ll be getting through the school day, and I am getting through it, but I feel wound up and discouraged in a way that really doesn’t feel like me. And then when I think about what’s coming up—lesson plans, emails, whatever needs to be done for this initiative—I sort of freeze or avoid it. Those are things I used to just sit down and handle without much thought, and now I find myself putting them off, which then gives me more to think about. So yes, it’s been taking up a lot more space in my head than I’d like.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious most of the time since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Most of the time might be a little strong, but… yes, I’ve felt much more wound up than usual. It’s not like I’m panicking all day or anything like that. I still show up, I still teach, I still do what I’m supposed to do during the day.\n\nBut ever since the district added this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on top of my regular teaching, I feel like I’m carrying this pressure around with me. I’m discouraged in a way that doesn’t really feel like me, and I notice I’m putting off things like lesson plans and emails—things I used to just sit down and do without turning them into this whole ordeal in my head.\n\nSo I don’t know if I’d say “anxious most of the time” exactly, but I do feel tense and preoccupied with it a lot of the time.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in your weight since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Not that I’ve really noticed, no. I mean, my clothes seem to fit about the same, and I haven’t been weighing myself regularly enough to say, “Oh, it’s exactly the same” or anything like that.\n\nI suppose with everything going on I haven’t been paying close attention to it, but nothing has stood out to me as a real weight change.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding certain situations or places because of how you've been feeling?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’m avoiding places, exactly. I still go to school, I still stand in front of the class, I still get through the teaching day. It’s more that I’ve started avoiding certain tasks connected to all of this, if that makes sense.\n\nSince the district put this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on my plate on top of my regular teaching, I notice I’ll put off sitting down with lesson plans or responding to emails related to it. Those are things I used to just do—maybe not joyfully, but without this big internal hurdle. Now I’ll find myself finding other things to do first, or telling myself I’ll get to it later, and then it’s hanging over me.\n\nSo no, not avoiding a building or a particular room or anything like that. It’s more avoiding the work that makes me feel wound up and discouraged.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt disconnected from your emotions or like you're just going through the motions of daily life without feeling much of anything?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in the sense of feeling numb or detached from myself. I still feel things pretty clearly—maybe too clearly sometimes, honestly. I know when I’m frustrated, or discouraged, or tense. I’m not walking around feeling blank or like I’m watching my life from the outside.\n\nThere are moments during the school day where I’m very much on autopilot, but that’s also kind of part of teaching sometimes, you know? You move from one thing to the next because the day demands it. But I wouldn’t say I feel emotionally disconnected. I’m present, I’m just… tired of having this particular pressure sitting there in the background.\n\nDoctor: Are there things you used to enjoy that you no longer find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I no longer enjoy things, exactly. I still can enjoy things when I’m actually doing them, if that makes sense. Like if I’m with my family, or if I’m watching something I like, or doing something quiet at home, I can still feel that it’s pleasant.\n\nI think what’s changed more is that it takes a little more effort to get myself into those things, because my mind is kind of pulled back toward school and what I still need to deal with. So sometimes something that should feel relaxing doesn’t feel as relaxing as it normally would, but I wouldn’t say the pleasure is gone.\n\nIt’s more like the stress is crowding the edges of things I normally enjoy, not that I can’t enjoy them at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things that you're not getting done or the way you're handling your responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do feel guilty about it, probably more than I’d like to admit. I’m present, I’m just… tired of having this particular pressure sitting there in the background.\n\nDoctor: Are there things you used to enjoy that you no longer find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I no longer enjoy things, exactly. I still can enjoy things when I’m actually doing them, if that makes sense. Like if I’m with my family, or if I’m watching something I like, or doing something quiet at home, I can still feel that it’s pleasant.\n\nI think what’s changed more is that it takes a little more effort to get myself into those things, because my mind is kind of pulled back toward school and what I still need to deal with. So sometimes something that should feel relaxing doesn’t feel as relaxing as it normally would, but I wouldn’t say the pleasure is gone.\n\nIt’s more like the stress is crowding the edges of things I normally enjoy, not that I can’t enjoy them at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things that you're not getting done or the way you're handling your responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do feel guilty about it, probably more than I’d like to admit. I’m still showing up, and I’m still teaching, and the kids are being taught—I don’t want to make it sound like I’ve stopped doing my job. The basic responsibilities are getting done.\n\nBut there are things around the planning and the rollout work that I’ve either missed the deadline for or pushed back longer than I should have. And that bothers me because I’m not usually that person. I’ve always been the one who has the materials ready, answers the email, turns things in when they’re due, keeps track of the moving pieces.\n\nLately I’ve had colleagues remind me about things I used to remember on my own, and even when they’re kind about it, I feel embarrassed. I’ll think, “Why didn’t I just do that already?” or “People are going to think I’m dropping the ball.” So yes, there’s definitely guilt there—not constant every second, but it hangs over me when I know I’ve let something slide.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you often feel like you're on edge and can't relax?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 56 / elementary_or_middle_school_teacher / graduate\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response\nmanifestation: \"I came in because ever since the district put this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on my plate on top of my regular teaching, I still get through the day, but I feel wound up and discouraged in a way that isn't like me, and I've started putting off lesson plans and emails I used to handle without a second thought.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, I do. Not every single minute, but a lot more than I’m used to. I feel like I’m kind of wound up most of the time, especially since the district put this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on me on top of my regular teaching. I can get through the school day, and I do, but there’s this pressure sitting there that doesn’t really let up.

Even when I’m technically done for the day, I’ll still be thinking about lesson plans I haven’t finished, or emails I should have answered, or some piece of the rollout that I’m supposed to be tracking. And then I have a hard time actually relaxing because it feels like there’s always something I’m behind on. It’s discouraging, honestly, because I’m usually pretty dependable with those things. I used to just handle lesson plans and emails without even thinking that much about it, and now I find myself putting them off because I feel overwhelmed before I even start.

So yes, I’d say I’m on edge. I’m functioning, but I don’t feel like myself.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.0, F32.1, F43.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because I just haven’t been feeling like myself lately. I’m a teacher, and the district put this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on my plate on top of my regular teaching responsibilities, and I know that probably sounds like just another school initiative, because there are always initiatives, but this one has really gotten under my skin.\n\nI’m still showing up, I’m still getting through the school day, I’m not falling apart or anything, but I feel wound up almost all the time and discouraged in a way that’s not typical for me. And I’ve noticed I’m putting off lesson plans and emails—things I used to just sit down and handle without even thinking much about it. Now I’ll stare at them, or tell myself I’ll do them later, and then they just sit there. That’s really not how I usually operate, so I thought maybe I should talk to someone before it gets worse.\n\nDoctor: Are you still enjoying activities or hobbies that you typically found pleasurable before feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that, honestly. I mean, I haven’t really sat down and taken inventory of that. I still do some of the things I normally do, or at least I intend to, but I don’t know if I’ve noticed a clear “I don’t enjoy anything anymore” kind of thing.\n\nIt’s more like everything has this layer of distraction on top of it, if that makes sense. Like if I’m doing something I usually like, part of my brain is still circling around school stuff or thinking about what I should be doing instead. So I wouldn’t say I can’t enjoy things, but I’m not sure I’m enjoying them in the same relaxed way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad most of the time since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say sad most of the time, exactly. Discouraged, yes. Wound up, definitely. It’s more like since the district added this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on top of my regular teaching, I’ve felt kind of unlike myself.\n\nI’m still getting through the day and doing my job, but there’s this constant pressure in the background, and I notice myself putting off lesson plans and emails that I used to just handle without making a whole production out of it. So it’s not that I’m crying all day or feeling deeply sad every minute. It’s more that I feel tense and worn down and kind of disheartened in a way that doesn’t feel normal for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about things that are coming up, like the school initiative or your teaching responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. I mean, I don’t know if I’d call it worrying in the sense of imagining disasters, exactly, but my mind keeps going back to it. Ever since the district put this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on me in addition to my regular teaching, it’s like there’s this constant hum in the background.\n\nI’ll be getting through the school day, and I am getting through it, but I feel wound up and discouraged in a way that really doesn’t feel like me. And then when I think about what’s coming up—lesson plans, emails, whatever needs to be done for this initiative—I sort of freeze or avoid it. Those are things I used to just sit down and handle without much thought, and now I find myself putting them off, which then gives me more to think about. So yes, it’s been taking up a lot more space in my head than I’d like.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious most of the time since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Most of the time might be a little strong, but… yes, I’ve felt much more wound up than usual. It’s not like I’m panicking all day or anything like that. I still show up, I still teach, I still do what I’m supposed to do during the day.\n\nBut ever since the district added this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on top of my regular teaching, I feel like I’m carrying this pressure around with me. I’m discouraged in a way that doesn’t really feel like me, and I notice I’m putting off things like lesson plans and emails—things I used to just sit down and do without turning them into this whole ordeal in my head.\n\nSo I don’t know if I’d say “anxious most of the time” exactly, but I do feel tense and preoccupied with it a lot of the time.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in your weight since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Not that I’ve really noticed, no. I mean, my clothes seem to fit about the same, and I haven’t been weighing myself regularly enough to say, “Oh, it’s exactly the same” or anything like that.\n\nI suppose with everything going on I haven’t been paying close attention to it, but nothing has stood out to me as a real weight change.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding certain situations or places because of how you've been feeling?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’m avoiding places, exactly. I still go to school, I still stand in front of the class, I still get through the teaching day. It’s more that I’ve started avoiding certain tasks connected to all of this, if that makes sense.\n\nSince the district put this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on my plate on top of my regular teaching, I notice I’ll put off sitting down with lesson plans or responding to emails related to it. Those are things I used to just do—maybe not joyfully, but without this big internal hurdle. Now I’ll find myself finding other things to do first, or telling myself I’ll get to it later, and then it’s hanging over me.\n\nSo no, not avoiding a building or a particular room or anything like that. It’s more avoiding the work that makes me feel wound up and discouraged.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt disconnected from your emotions or like you're just going through the motions of daily life without feeling much of anything?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in the sense of feeling numb or detached from myself. I still feel things pretty clearly—maybe too clearly sometimes, honestly. I know when I’m frustrated, or discouraged, or tense. I’m not walking around feeling blank or like I’m watching my life from the outside.\n\nThere are moments during the school day where I’m very much on autopilot, but that’s also kind of part of teaching sometimes, you know? You move from one thing to the next because the day demands it. But I wouldn’t say I feel emotionally disconnected. I’m present, I’m just… tired of having this particular pressure sitting there in the background.\n\nDoctor: Are there things you used to enjoy that you no longer find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I no longer enjoy things, exactly. I still can enjoy things when I’m actually doing them, if that makes sense. I’ve always been the one who has the materials ready, answers the email, turns things in when they’re due, keeps track of the moving pieces.\n\nLately I’ve had colleagues remind me about things I used to remember on my own, and even when they’re kind about it, I feel embarrassed. I’ll think, “Why didn’t I just do that already?” or “People are going to think I’m dropping the ball.” So yes, there’s definitely guilt there—not constant every second, but it hangs over me when I know I’ve let something slide.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're on edge and can't relax?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Not every single minute, but a lot more than I’m used to. I feel like I’m kind of wound up most of the time, especially since the district put this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on me on top of my regular teaching. I can get through the school day, and I do, but there’s this pressure sitting there that doesn’t really let up.\n\nEven when I’m technically done for the day, I’ll still be thinking about lesson plans I haven’t finished, or emails I should have answered, or some piece of the rollout that I’m supposed to be tracking. And then I have a hard time actually relaxing because it feels like there’s always something I’m behind on. It’s discouraging, honestly, because I’m usually pretty dependable with those things. I used to just handle lesson plans and emails without even thinking that much about it, and now I find myself putting them off because I feel overwhelmed before I even start.\n\nSo yes, I’d say I’m on edge. I’m functioning, but I don’t feel like myself.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you lost a significant amount of weight since you started feeling this way?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "patient_turn_index: 12\n(If 1, this is your first reply — return your chief complaint.)\nReturn the JSON now."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
{"key":"None","name":"None","description":"None","reason":"The doctor is asking about significant weight loss, but the provided symptom profile does not include appetite or weight changes."}

Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Are there things you used to enjoy that you no longer find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I no longer enjoy things, exactly. I still can enjoy things when I’m actually doing them, if that makes sense. Like if I’m with my family, or if I’m watching something I like, or doing something quiet at home, I can still feel that it’s pleasant.\n\nI think what’s changed more is that it takes a little more effort to get myself into those things, because my mind is kind of pulled back toward school and what I still need to deal with. So sometimes something that should feel relaxing doesn’t feel as relaxing as it normally would, but I wouldn’t say the pleasure is gone.\n\nIt’s more like the stress is crowding the edges of things I normally enjoy, not that I can’t enjoy them at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things that you're not getting done or the way you're handling your responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do feel guilty about it, probably more than I’d like to admit. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. I don’t think so, no. I mean, I haven’t been tracking my weight carefully or anything like that, so I don’t want to say absolutely, but I haven’t noticed my clothes fitting differently or anyone commenting that I look thinner.

    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.0, F32.1, F43.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because I just haven’t been feeling like myself lately. I’m a teacher, and the district put this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on my plate on top of my regular teaching responsibilities, and I know that probably sounds like just another school initiative, because there are always initiatives, but this one has really gotten under my skin.\n\nI’m still showing up, I’m still getting through the school day, I’m not falling apart or anything, but I feel wound up almost all the time and discouraged in a way that’s not typical for me. And I’ve noticed I’m putting off lesson plans and emails—things I used to just sit down and handle without even thinking much about it. Now I’ll stare at them, or tell myself I’ll do them later, and then they just sit there. That’s really not how I usually operate, so I thought maybe I should talk to someone before it gets worse.\n\nDoctor: Are you still enjoying activities or hobbies that you typically found pleasurable before feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that, honestly. I mean, I haven’t really sat down and taken inventory of that. I still do some of the things I normally do, or at least I intend to, but I don’t know if I’ve noticed a clear “I don’t enjoy anything anymore” kind of thing.\n\nIt’s more like everything has this layer of distraction on top of it, if that makes sense. Like if I’m doing something I usually like, part of my brain is still circling around school stuff or thinking about what I should be doing instead. So I wouldn’t say I can’t enjoy things, but I’m not sure I’m enjoying them in the same relaxed way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad most of the time since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say sad most of the time, exactly. Discouraged, yes. Wound up, definitely. It’s more like since the district added this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on top of my regular teaching, I’ve felt kind of unlike myself.\n\nI’m still getting through the day and doing my job, but there’s this constant pressure in the background, and I notice myself putting off lesson plans and emails that I used to just handle without making a whole production out of it. So it’s not that I’m crying all day or feeling deeply sad every minute. It’s more that I feel tense and worn down and kind of disheartened in a way that doesn’t feel normal for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about things that are coming up, like the school initiative or your teaching responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. I mean, I don’t know if I’d call it worrying in the sense of imagining disasters, exactly, but my mind keeps going back to it. Ever since the district put this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on me in addition to my regular teaching, it’s like there’s this constant hum in the background.\n\nI’ll be getting through the school day, and I am getting through it, but I feel wound up and discouraged in a way that really doesn’t feel like me. And then when I think about what’s coming up—lesson plans, emails, whatever needs to be done for this initiative—I sort of freeze or avoid it. Those are things I used to just sit down and handle without much thought, and now I find myself putting them off, which then gives me more to think about. So yes, it’s been taking up a lot more space in my head than I’d like.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious most of the time since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Most of the time might be a little strong, but… yes, I’ve felt much more wound up than usual. It’s not like I’m panicking all day or anything like that. I still show up, I still teach, I still do what I’m supposed to do during the day.\n\nBut ever since the district added this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on top of my regular teaching, I feel like I’m carrying this pressure around with me. I’m discouraged in a way that doesn’t really feel like me, and I notice I’m putting off things like lesson plans and emails—things I used to just sit down and do without turning them into this whole ordeal in my head.\n\nSo I don’t know if I’d say “anxious most of the time” exactly, but I do feel tense and preoccupied with it a lot of the time.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in your weight since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Not that I’ve really noticed, no. I mean, my clothes seem to fit about the same, and I haven’t been weighing myself regularly enough to say, “Oh, it’s exactly the same” or anything like that.\n\nI suppose with everything going on I haven’t been paying close attention to it, but nothing has stood out to me as a real weight change.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding certain situations or places because of how you've been feeling?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’m avoiding places, exactly. I still go to school, I still stand in front of the class, I still get through the teaching day. It’s more that I’ve started avoiding certain tasks connected to all of this, if that makes sense.\n\nSince the district put this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on my plate on top of my regular teaching, I notice I’ll put off sitting down with lesson plans or responding to emails related to it. Those are things I used to just do—maybe not joyfully, but without this big internal hurdle. Now I’ll find myself finding other things to do first, or telling myself I’ll get to it later, and then it’s hanging over me.\n\nSo no, not avoiding a building or a particular room or anything like that. It’s more avoiding the work that makes me feel wound up and discouraged.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt disconnected from your emotions or like you're just going through the motions of daily life without feeling much of anything?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in the sense of feeling numb or detached from myself. I still feel things pretty clearly—maybe too clearly sometimes, honestly. I know when I’m frustrated, or discouraged, or tense. I’m not walking around feeling blank or like I’m watching my life from the outside.\n\nThere are moments during the school day where I’m very much on autopilot, but that’s also kind of part of teaching sometimes, you know? You move from one thing to the next because the day demands it. But I wouldn’t say I feel emotionally disconnected. I’m present, I’m just… tired of having this particular pressure sitting there in the background.\n\nDoctor: Are there things you used to enjoy that you no longer find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I no longer enjoy things, exactly. I still can enjoy things when I’m actually doing them, if that makes sense. Like if I’m with my family, or if I’m watching something I like, or doing something quiet at home, I can still feel that it’s pleasant.\n\nI think what’s changed more is that it takes a little more effort to get myself into those things, because my mind is kind of pulled back toward school and what I still need to deal with. So sometimes something that should feel relaxing doesn’t feel as relaxing as it normally would, but I wouldn’t say the pleasure is gone.\n\nIt’s more like the stress is crowding the edges of things I normally enjoy, not that I can’t enjoy them at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things that you're not getting done or the way you're handling your responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do feel guilty about it, probably more than I’d like to admit. I’m still showing up, and I’m still teaching, and the kids are being taught—I don’t want to make it sound like I’ve stopped doing my job. The basic responsibilities are getting done.\n\nBut there are things around the planning and the rollout work that I’ve either missed the deadline for or pushed back longer than I should have. And that bothers me because I’m not usually that person. I’ve always been the one who has the materials ready, answers the email, turns things in when they’re due, keeps track of the moving pieces.\n\nLately I’ve had colleagues remind me about things I used to remember on my own, and even when they’re kind about it, I feel embarrassed. I’ll think, “Why didn’t I just do that already?” or “People are going to think I’m dropping the ball.” So yes, there’s definitely guilt there—not constant every second, but it hangs over me when I know I’ve let something slide.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're on edge and can't relax?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Not every single minute, but a lot more than I’m used to. I feel like I’m kind of wound up most of the time, especially since the district put this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on me on top of my regular teaching. I can get through the school day, and I do, but there’s this pressure sitting there that doesn’t really let up.\n\nEven when I’m technically done for the day, I’ll still be thinking about lesson plans I haven’t finished, or emails I should have answered, or some piece of the rollout that I’m supposed to be tracking. And then I have a hard time actually relaxing because it feels like there’s always something I’m behind on. It’s discouraging, honestly, because I’m usually pretty dependable with those things. I used to just handle lesson plans and emails without even thinking that much about it, and now I find myself putting them off because I feel overwhelmed before I even start.\n\nSo yes, I’d say I’m on edge. I’m functioning, but I don’t feel like myself.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. I mean, I haven’t been tracking my weight carefully or anything like that, so I don’t want to say absolutely, but I haven’t noticed my clothes fitting differently or anyone commenting that I look thinner.\n\nMy eating has maybe been a little less organized just because I’m busy and distracted, but in terms of a significant amount of weight loss, no, I don’t think that’s happened.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I wouldn’t say sad most of the day, every day, no. It’s more that I feel discouraged and wound up, if that makes sense. I came in because this really started after the district put the bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on my plate on top of my regular teaching, and since then I just haven’t felt like myself.

I’m still getting through the day. I’m still showing up, teaching, doing what needs to be done in the classroom. But there’s this heaviness around the work now, especially with things I used to handle automatically—lesson plans, emails, keeping track of what needs to go where. I’ve started putting some of that off, which is very unlike me, and then I feel worse because I know I’m not operating the way I normally do.

So, not sad all day every day exactly. More stressed, discouraged, and kind of worn down by it.

I mean, I haven’t really sat down and taken inventory of that. Like if I’m doing something I usually like, part of my brain is still circling around school stuff or thinking about what I should be doing instead. So it’s not that I’m crying all day or feeling deeply sad every minute. I mean, I don’t know if I’d call it worrying in the sense of imagining disasters, exactly, but my mind keeps going back to it. Ever since the district put this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on me in addition to my regular teaching, it’s like there’s this constant hum in the background.\n\nI’ll be getting through the school day, and I am getting through it, but I feel wound up and discouraged in a way that really doesn’t feel like me. And then when I think about what’s coming up—lesson plans, emails, whatever needs to be done for this initiative—I sort of freeze or avoid it. Those are things I used to just sit down and handle without much thought, and now I find myself putting them off, which then gives me more to think about. So yes, it’s been taking up a lot more space in my head than I’d like.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious most of the time since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Most of the time might be a little strong, but… yes, I’ve felt much more wound up than usual. It’s not like I’m panicking all day or anything like that. I still show up, I still teach, I still do what I’m supposed to do during the day.\n\nBut ever since the district added this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on top of my regular teaching, I feel like I’m carrying this pressure around with me. I’m discouraged in a way that doesn’t really feel like me, and I notice I’m putting off things like lesson plans and emails—things I used to just sit down and do without turning them into this whole ordeal in my head.\n\nSo I don’t know if I’d say “anxious most of the time” exactly, but I do feel tense and preoccupied with it a lot of the time.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in your weight since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Not that I’ve really noticed, no. I mean, my clothes seem to fit about the same, and I haven’t been weighing myself regularly enough to say, “Oh, it’s exactly the same” or anything like that.\n\nI suppose with everything going on I haven’t been paying close attention to it, but nothing has stood out to me as a real weight change.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding certain situations or places because of how you've been feeling?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’m avoiding places, exactly. I still go to school, I still stand in front of the class, I still get through the teaching day. It’s more that I’ve started avoiding certain tasks connected to all of this, if that makes sense.\n\nSince the district put this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on my plate on top of my regular teaching, I notice I’ll put off sitting down with lesson plans or responding to emails related to it. Those are things I used to just do—maybe not joyfully, but without this big internal hurdle. Now I’ll find myself finding other things to do first, or telling myself I’ll get to it later, and then it’s hanging over me.\n\nSo no, not avoiding a building or a particular room or anything like that. It’s more avoiding the work that makes me feel wound up and discouraged.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt disconnected from your emotions or like you're just going through the motions of daily life without feeling much of anything?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in the sense of feeling numb or detached from myself. I still feel things pretty clearly—maybe too clearly sometimes, honestly. I know when I’m frustrated, or discouraged, or tense. I’m not walking around feeling blank or like I’m watching my life from the outside.\n\nThere are moments during the school day where I’m very much on autopilot, but that’s also kind of part of teaching sometimes, you know? You move from one thing to the next because the day demands it. But I wouldn’t say I feel emotionally disconnected. I’m present, I’m just… tired of having this particular pressure sitting there in the background.\n\nDoctor: Are there things you used to enjoy that you no longer find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I no longer enjoy things, exactly. I still can enjoy things when I’m actually doing them, if that makes sense. Like if I’m with my family, or if I’m watching something I like, or doing something quiet at home, I can still feel that it’s pleasant.\n\nI think what’s changed more is that it takes a little more effort to get myself into those things, because my mind is kind of pulled back toward school and what I still need to deal with. I still show up, I still teach, I still do what I’m supposed to do during the day.\n\nBut ever since the district added this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on top of my regular teaching, I feel like I’m carrying this pressure around with me. I’m discouraged in a way that doesn’t really feel like me, and I notice I’m putting off things like lesson plans and emails—things I used to just sit down and do without turning them into this whole ordeal in my head.\n\nSo I don’t know if I’d say “anxious most of the time” exactly, but I do feel tense and preoccupied with it a lot of the time.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in your weight since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Not that I’ve really noticed, no. I mean, my clothes seem to fit about the same, and I haven’t been weighing myself regularly enough to say, “Oh, it’s exactly the same” or anything like that.\n\nI suppose with everything going on I haven’t been paying close attention to it, but nothing has stood out to me as a real weight change.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding certain situations or places because of how you've been feeling?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’m avoiding places, exactly. I still go to school, I still stand in front of the class, I still get through the teaching day. It’s more that I’ve started avoiding certain tasks connected to all of this, if that makes sense.\n\nSince the district put this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on my plate on top of my regular teaching, I notice I’ll put off sitting down with lesson plans or responding to emails related to it. Those are things I used to just do—maybe not joyfully, but without this big internal hurdle. Now I’ll find myself finding other things to do first, or telling myself I’ll get to it later, and then it’s hanging over me.\n\nSo no, not avoiding a building or a particular room or anything like that. It’s more avoiding the work that makes me feel wound up and discouraged.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt disconnected from your emotions or like you're just going through the motions of daily life without feeling much of anything?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in the sense of feeling numb or detached from myself. I still feel things pretty clearly—maybe too clearly sometimes, honestly. I know when I’m frustrated, or discouraged, or tense. I’m not walking around feeling blank or like I’m watching my life from the outside.\n\nThere are moments during the school day where I’m very much on autopilot, but that’s also kind of part of teaching sometimes, you know? You move from one thing to the next because the day demands it. But I wouldn’t say I feel emotionally disconnected. I’m present, I’m just… tired of having this particular pressure sitting there in the background.\n\nDoctor: Are there things you used to enjoy that you no longer find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I no longer enjoy things, exactly. I still can enjoy things when I’m actually doing them, if that makes sense. Like if I’m with my family, or if I’m watching something I like, or doing something quiet at home, I can still feel that it’s pleasant.\n\nI think what’s changed more is that it takes a little more effort to get myself into those things, because my mind is kind of pulled back toward school and what I still need to deal with. So sometimes something that should feel relaxing doesn’t feel as relaxing as it normally would, but I wouldn’t say the pleasure is gone.\n\nIt’s more like the stress is crowding the edges of things I normally enjoy, not that I can’t enjoy them at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things that you're not getting done or the way you're handling your responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do feel guilty about it, probably more than I’d like to admit. I’m still showing up, and I’m still teaching, and the kids are being taught—I don’t want to make it sound like I’ve stopped doing my job. The basic responsibilities are getting done.\n\nBut there are things around the planning and the rollout work that I’ve either missed the deadline for or pushed back longer than I should have. And that bothers me because I’m not usually that person. I’ve always been the one who has the materials ready, answers the email, turns things in when they’re due, keeps track of the moving pieces.\n\nLately I’ve had colleagues remind me about things I used to remember on my own, and even when they’re kind about it, I feel embarrassed. I’ll think, “Why didn’t I just do that already?” or “People are going to think I’m dropping the ball.” So yes, there’s definitely guilt there—not constant every second, but it hangs over me when I know I’ve let something slide.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're on edge and can't relax?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Not every single minute, but a lot more than I’m used to. I feel like I’m kind of wound up most of the time, especially since the district put this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on me on top of my regular teaching. I can get through the school day, and I do, but there’s this pressure sitting there that doesn’t really let up.\n\nEven when I’m technically done for the day, I’ll still be thinking about lesson plans I haven’t finished, or emails I should have answered, or some piece of the rollout that I’m supposed to be tracking. And then I have a hard time actually relaxing because it feels like there’s always something I’m behind on. It’s discouraging, honestly, because I’m usually pretty dependable with those things. I used to just handle lesson plans and emails without even thinking that much about it, and now I find myself putting them off because I feel overwhelmed before I even start.\n\nSo yes, I’d say I’m on edge. I’m functioning, but I don’t feel like myself.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. I mean, I haven’t been tracking my weight carefully or anything like that, so I don’t want to say absolutely, but I haven’t noticed my clothes fitting differently or anyone commenting that I look thinner.\n\nMy eating has maybe been a little less organized just because I’m busy and distracted, but in terms of a significant amount of weight loss, no, I don’t think that’s happened.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad most of the day, every day?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say sad most of the day, every day, no. It’s more that I feel discouraged and wound up, if that makes sense. I came in because this really started after the district put the bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on my plate on top of my regular teaching, and since then I just haven’t felt like myself.\n\nI’m still getting through the day. I’m still showing up, teaching, doing what needs to be done in the classroom. But there’s this heaviness around the work now, especially with things I used to handle automatically—lesson plans, emails, keeping track of what needs to go where. I’ve started putting some of that off, which is very unlike me, and then I feel worse because I know I’m not operating the way I normally do.\n\nSo, not sad all day every day exactly. More stressed, discouraged, and kind of worn down by it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

I’m not completely sure how to answer that, because I don’t think it’s like I’ve stopped liking things, exactly. I still know what I enjoy, if that makes sense. It’s more that I don’t always get to them, or when I do, I’m not as able to settle into them because my mind is still kind of busy.

Like, if you asked me, “Do you still enjoy the things you used to enjoy?” I’d say yes, in theory. I haven’t had this feeling of, “Nothing matters” or “I don’t care about anything anymore.” It’s more that I’m distracted, and it takes me longer to relax enough to enjoy something. Sometimes I’ll start doing something I normally like and then realize I’m thinking about what I should be doing instead.

So I wouldn’t say I’ve lost interest, not in a big or complete way. It’s more that stress is crowding things out.

I’m not totally sure how to separate that out, honestly. I mean, there are nights where my mind is still going when I get into bed, and work can be part of that, but I don’t know that I’d say it’s often in a clear, consistent way.
