It’s more like since the district added this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on top of my regular teaching, I’ve felt kind of unlike myself.\n\nI’m still getting through the day and doing my job, but there’s this constant pressure in the background, and I notice myself putting off lesson plans and emails that I used to just handle without making a whole production out of it. So it’s not that I’m crying all day or feeling deeply sad every minute. It’s more that I feel tense and worn down and kind of disheartened in a way that doesn’t feel normal for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about things that are coming up, like the school initiative or your teaching responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. I mean, I don’t know if I’d call it worrying in the sense of imagining disasters, exactly, but my mind keeps going back to it. Ever since the district put this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on me in addition to my regular teaching, it’s like there’s this constant hum in the background.\n\nI’ll be getting through the school day, and I am getting through it, but I feel wound up and discouraged in a way that really doesn’t feel like me. And then when I think about what’s coming up—lesson plans, emails, whatever needs to be done for this initiative—I sort of freeze or avoid it. Those are things I used to just sit down and handle without much thought, and now I find myself putting them off, which then gives me more to think about. So yes, it’s been taking up a lot more space in my head than I’d like.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious most of the time since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Most of the time might be a little strong, but… yes, I’ve felt much more wound up than usual. It’s not like I’m panicking all day or anything like that. I still show up, I still teach, I still do what I’m supposed to do during the day.\n\nBut ever since the district added this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on top of my regular teaching, I feel like I’m carrying this pressure around with me. I’m discouraged in a way that doesn’t really feel like me, and I notice I’m putting off things like lesson plans and emails—things I used to just sit down and do without turning them into this whole ordeal in my head.\n\nSo I don’t know if I’d say “anxious most of the time” exactly, but I do feel tense and preoccupied with it a lot of the time.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in your weight since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Not that I’ve really noticed, no. I mean, my clothes seem to fit about the same, and I haven’t been weighing myself regularly enough to say, “Oh, it’s exactly the same” or anything like that.\n\nI suppose with everything going on I haven’t been paying close attention to it, but nothing has stood out to me as a real weight change.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding certain situations or places because of how you've been feeling?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’m avoiding places, exactly. I still go to school, I still stand in front of the class, I still get through the teaching day. It’s more that I’ve started avoiding certain tasks connected to all of this, if that makes sense.\n\nSince the district put this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on my plate on top of my regular teaching, I notice I’ll put off sitting down with lesson plans or responding to emails related to it. Those are things I used to just do—maybe not joyfully, but without this big internal hurdle. Now I’ll find myself finding other things to do first, or telling myself I’ll get to it later, and then it’s hanging over me.\n\nSo no, not avoiding a building or a particular room or anything like that. It’s more avoiding the work that makes me feel wound up and discouraged.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt disconnected from your emotions or like you're just going through the motions of daily life without feeling much of anything?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in the sense of feeling numb or detached from myself. I still feel things pretty clearly—maybe too clearly sometimes, honestly. I know when I’m frustrated, or discouraged, or tense. I’m not walking around feeling blank or like I’m watching my life from the outside.\n\nThere are moments during the school day where I’m very much on autopilot, but that’s also kind of part of teaching sometimes, you know? You move from one thing to the next because the day demands it. But I wouldn’t say I feel emotionally disconnected. I’m present, I’m just… tired of having this particular pressure sitting there in the background.\n\nDoctor: Are there things you used to enjoy that you no longer find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I no longer enjoy things, exactly. I still can enjoy things when I’m actually doing them, if that makes sense. Like if I’m with my family, or if I’m watching something I like, or doing something quiet at home, I can still feel that it’s pleasant.\n\nI think what’s changed more is that it takes a little more effort to get myself into those things, because my mind is kind of pulled back toward school and what I still need to deal with. So sometimes something that should feel relaxing doesn’t feel as relaxing as it normally would, but I wouldn’t say the pleasure is gone.\n\nIt’s more like the stress is crowding the edges of things I normally enjoy, not that I can’t enjoy them at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things that you're not getting done or the way you're handling your responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do feel guilty about it, probably more than I’d like to admit. I’m still showing up, and I’m still teaching, and the kids are being taught—I don’t want to make it sound like I’ve stopped doing my job. The basic responsibilities are getting done.\n\nBut there are things around the planning and the rollout work that I’ve either missed the deadline for or pushed back longer than I should have. And that bothers me because I’m not usually that person. I’ve always been the one who has the materials ready, answers the email, turns things in when they’re due, keeps track of the moving pieces.\n\nLately I’ve had colleagues remind me about things I used to remember on my own, and even when they’re kind about it, I feel embarrassed. I’ll think, “Why didn’t I just do that already?” or “People are going to think I’m dropping the ball.” So yes, there’s definitely guilt there—not constant every second, but it hangs over me when I know I’ve let something slide.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're on edge and can't relax?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Not every single minute, but a lot more than I’m used to. I feel like I’m kind of wound up most of the time, especially since the district put this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on me on top of my regular teaching. I can get through the school day, and I do, but there’s this pressure sitting there that doesn’t really let up.\n\nEven when I’m technically done for the day, I’ll still be thinking about lesson plans I haven’t finished, or emails I should have answered, or some piece of the rollout that I’m supposed to be tracking. And then I have a hard time actually relaxing because it feels like there’s always something I’m behind on. It’s discouraging, honestly, because I’m usually pretty dependable with those things. I used to just handle lesson plans and emails without even thinking that much about it, and now I find myself putting them off because I feel overwhelmed before I even start.\n\nSo yes, I’d say I’m on edge. I’m functioning, but I don’t feel like myself.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. I mean, I haven’t been tracking my weight carefully or anything like that, so I don’t want to say absolutely, but I haven’t noticed my clothes fitting differently or anyone commenting that I look thinner.\n\nMy eating has maybe been a little less organized just because I’m busy and distracted, but in terms of a significant amount of weight loss, no, I don’t think that’s happened.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad most of the day, every day?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say sad most of the day, every day, no. It’s more that I feel discouraged and wound up, if that makes sense. I came in because this really started after the district put the bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on my plate on top of my regular teaching, and since then I just haven’t felt like myself.\n\nI’m still getting through the day. I’m still showing up, teaching, doing what needs to be done in the classroom. But there’s this heaviness around the work now, especially with things I used to handle automatically—lesson plans, emails, keeping track of what needs to go where. I’ve started putting some of that off, which is very unlike me, and then I feel worse because I know I’m not operating the way I normally do.\n\nSo, not sad all day every day exactly. More stressed, discouraged, and kind of worn down by it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that, because I don’t think it’s like I’ve stopped liking things, exactly. I still know what I enjoy, if that makes sense. It’s more that I don’t always get to them, or when I do, I’m not as able to settle into them because my mind is still kind of busy.\n\nLike, if you asked me, “Do you still enjoy the things you used to enjoy?” I’d say yes, in theory. I haven’t had this feeling of, “Nothing matters” or “I don’t care about anything anymore.” It’s more that I’m distracted, and it takes me longer to relax enough to enjoy something. Sometimes I’ll start doing something I normally like and then realize I’m thinking about what I should be doing instead.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I’ve lost interest, not in a big or complete way. It’s more that stress is crowding things out.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble falling or staying asleep because you're thinking about work or other things?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to separate that out, honestly. I mean, there are nights where my mind is still going when I get into bed, and work can be part of that, but I don’t know that I’d say it’s often in a clear, consistent way.\n\nSometimes I’ll lie there and think, “Did I respond to that email?” or “What do I need to do tomorrow?” and then I have to kind of remind myself that I’m in bed and there’s nothing I can do about it right then. But I haven’t really been keeping track of how many nights that happens, or whether it’s the main reason I’m not sleeping well.\n\nSo… maybe occasionally, but I’m not sure I’d describe it as a major or constant problem. It’s more that my brain takes a while to quiet down sometimes.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

It’s more that I feel discouraged and wound up, if that makes sense. I came in because this really started after the district put the bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on my plate on top of my regular teaching, and since then I just haven’t felt like myself.\n\nI’m still getting through the day. But there’s this heaviness around the work now, especially with things I used to handle automatically—lesson plans, emails, keeping track of what needs to go where. More stressed, discouraged, and kind of worn down by it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that, because I don’t think it’s like I’ve stopped liking things, exactly. I still know what I enjoy, if that makes sense. It’s more that I don’t always get to them, or when I do, I’m not as able to settle into them because my mind is still kind of busy.\n\nLike, if you asked me, “Do you still enjoy the things you used to enjoy?” I’d say yes, in theory. I haven’t had this feeling of, “Nothing matters” or “I don’t care about anything anymore.” It’s more that I’m distracted, and it takes me longer to relax enough to enjoy something. It’s more that stress is crowding things out.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble falling or staying asleep because you're thinking about work or other things?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to separate that out, honestly. I mean, there are nights where my mind is still going when I get into bed, and work can be part of that, but I don’t know that I’d say it’s often in a clear, consistent way.\n\nSometimes I’ll lie there and think, “Did I respond to that email?” or “What do I need to do tomorrow?” and then I have to kind of remind myself that I’m in bed and there’s nothing I can do about it right then. It’s more that my brain takes a while to quiet down sometimes.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you find yourself worrying about things even when there's no apparent reason to worry?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 56 / elementary_or_middle_school_teacher / graduate\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there.     "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know that I would put it that way, exactly. I mean, I do worry, but usually I can connect it to something, even if the thing is maybe bigger in my head than it needs to be at the moment.

Like, I’m not just sitting there out of nowhere thinking something terrible is going to happen for no reason. It’s more that if there’s something unresolved, or something I’m not sure I handled correctly, my mind can circle back to it. But there’s usually a reason I can point to, even if I know I may be dwelling on it too much.

So… I don’t think I’m worrying for no apparent reason, no. It feels more tied to specific things.

    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.0, F32.1, F43.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because I just haven’t been feeling like myself lately. I’m a teacher, and the district put this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on my plate on top of my regular teaching responsibilities, and I know that probably sounds like just another school initiative, because there are always initiatives, but this one has really gotten under my skin.\n\nI’m still showing up, I’m still getting through the school day, I’m not falling apart or anything, but I feel wound up almost all the time and discouraged in a way that’s not typical for me. And I’ve noticed I’m putting off lesson plans and emails—things I used to just sit down and handle without even thinking much about it. Now I’ll stare at them, or tell myself I’ll do them later, and then they just sit there. That’s really not how I usually operate, so I thought maybe I should talk to someone before it gets worse.\n\nDoctor: Are you still enjoying activities or hobbies that you typically found pleasurable before feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that, honestly. I mean, I haven’t really sat down and taken inventory of that. I still do some of the things I normally do, or at least I intend to, but I don’t know if I’ve noticed a clear “I don’t enjoy anything anymore” kind of thing.\n\nIt’s more like everything has this layer of distraction on top of it, if that makes sense. Like if I’m doing something I usually like, part of my brain is still circling around school stuff or thinking about what I should be doing instead. So I wouldn’t say I can’t enjoy things, but I’m not sure I’m enjoying them in the same relaxed way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad most of the time since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say sad most of the time, exactly. Discouraged, yes. Wound up, definitely. It’s more like since the district added this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on top of my regular teaching, I’ve felt kind of unlike myself.\n\nI’m still getting through the day and doing my job, but there’s this constant pressure in the background, and I notice myself putting off lesson plans and emails that I used to just handle without making a whole production out of it. So it’s not that I’m crying all day or feeling deeply sad every minute. It’s more that I feel tense and worn down and kind of disheartened in a way that doesn’t feel normal for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about things that are coming up, like the school initiative or your teaching responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. I mean, I don’t know if I’d call it worrying in the sense of imagining disasters, exactly, but my mind keeps going back to it. Ever since the district put this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on me in addition to my regular teaching, it’s like there’s this constant hum in the background.\n\nI’ll be getting through the school day, and I am getting through it, but I feel wound up and discouraged in a way that really doesn’t feel like me. And then when I think about what’s coming up—lesson plans, emails, whatever needs to be done for this initiative—I sort of freeze or avoid it. Those are things I used to just sit down and handle without much thought, and now I find myself putting them off, which then gives me more to think about. So yes, it’s been taking up a lot more space in my head than I’d like.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious most of the time since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Most of the time might be a little strong, but… yes, I’ve felt much more wound up than usual. It’s not like I’m panicking all day or anything like that. I still show up, I still teach, I still do what I’m supposed to do during the day.\n\nBut ever since the district added this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on top of my regular teaching, I feel like I’m carrying this pressure around with me. I’m discouraged in a way that doesn’t really feel like me, and I notice I’m putting off things like lesson plans and emails—things I used to just sit down and do without turning them into this whole ordeal in my head.\n\nSo I don’t know if I’d say “anxious most of the time” exactly, but I do feel tense and preoccupied with it a lot of the time.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in your weight since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Not that I’ve really noticed, no. I mean, my clothes seem to fit about the same, and I haven’t been weighing myself regularly enough to say, “Oh, it’s exactly the same” or anything like that.\n\nI suppose with everything going on I haven’t been paying close attention to it, but nothing has stood out to me as a real weight change.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding certain situations or places because of how you've been feeling?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’m avoiding places, exactly. I still go to school, I still stand in front of the class, I still get through the teaching day. It’s more that I’ve started avoiding certain tasks connected to all of this, if that makes sense.\n\nSince the district put this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on my plate on top of my regular teaching, I notice I’ll put off sitting down with lesson plans or responding to emails related to it. Those are things I used to just do—maybe not joyfully, but without this big internal hurdle. Now I’ll find myself finding other things to do first, or telling myself I’ll get to it later, and then it’s hanging over me.\n\nSo no, not avoiding a building or a particular room or anything like that. It’s more avoiding the work that makes me feel wound up and discouraged.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt disconnected from your emotions or like you're just going through the motions of daily life without feeling much of anything?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in the sense of feeling numb or detached from myself. I still feel things pretty clearly—maybe too clearly sometimes, honestly. I know when I’m frustrated, or discouraged, or tense. I’m not walking around feeling blank or like I’m watching my life from the outside.\n\nThere are moments during the school day where I’m very much on autopilot, but that’s also kind of part of teaching sometimes, you know? You move from one thing to the next because the day demands it. But I wouldn’t say I feel emotionally disconnected. I’m present, I’m just… tired of having this particular pressure sitting there in the background.\n\nDoctor: Are there things you used to enjoy that you no longer find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I no longer enjoy things, exactly. I still can enjoy things when I’m actually doing them, if that makes sense. Like if I’m with my family, or if I’m watching something I like, or doing something quiet at home, I can still feel that it’s pleasant.\n\nI think what’s changed more is that it takes a little more effort to get myself into those things, because my mind is kind of pulled back toward school and what I still need to deal with. So sometimes something that should feel relaxing doesn’t feel as relaxing as it normally would, but I wouldn’t say the pleasure is gone.\n\nIt’s more like the stress is crowding the edges of things I normally enjoy, not that I can’t enjoy them at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things that you're not getting done or the way you're handling your responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do feel guilty about it, probably more than I’d like to admit. I’m still showing up, and I’m still teaching, and the kids are being taught—I don’t want to make it sound like I’ve stopped doing my job. The basic responsibilities are getting done.\n\nBut there are things around the planning and the rollout work that I’ve either missed the deadline for or pushed back longer than I should have. And that bothers me because I’m not usually that person. I’ve always been the one who has the materials ready, answers the email, turns things in when they’re due, keeps track of the moving pieces.\n\nLately I’ve had colleagues remind me about things I used to remember on my own, and even when they’re kind about it, I feel embarrassed. I’ll think, “Why didn’t I just do that already?” or “People are going to think I’m dropping the ball.” So yes, there’s definitely guilt there—not constant every second, but it hangs over me when I know I’ve let something slide.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're on edge and can't relax?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Not every single minute, but a lot more than I’m used to. I feel like I’m kind of wound up most of the time, especially since the district put this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on me on top of my regular teaching. I can get through the school day, and I do, but there’s this pressure sitting there that doesn’t really let up.\n\nEven when I’m technically done for the day, I’ll still be thinking about lesson plans I haven’t finished, or emails I should have answered, or some piece of the rollout that I’m supposed to be tracking. And then I have a hard time actually relaxing because it feels like there’s always something I’m behind on. It’s discouraging, honestly, because I’m usually pretty dependable with those things. I used to just handle lesson plans and emails without even thinking that much about it, and now I find myself putting them off because I feel overwhelmed before I even start.\n\nSo yes, I’d say I’m on edge. I’m functioning, but I don’t feel like myself.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. I mean, I haven’t been tracking my weight carefully or anything like that, so I don’t want to say absolutely, but I haven’t noticed my clothes fitting differently or anyone commenting that I look thinner.\n\nMy eating has maybe been a little less organized just because I’m busy and distracted, but in terms of a significant amount of weight loss, no, I don’t think that’s happened.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad most of the day, every day?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say sad most of the day, every day, no. It’s more that I feel discouraged and wound up, if that makes sense. I came in because this really started after the district put the bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on my plate on top of my regular teaching, and since then I just haven’t felt like myself.\n\nI’m still getting through the day. I’m still showing up, teaching, doing what needs to be done in the classroom. But there’s this heaviness around the work now, especially with things I used to handle automatically—lesson plans, emails, keeping track of what needs to go where. More stressed, discouraged, and kind of worn down by it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that, because I don’t think it’s like I’ve stopped liking things, exactly. It’s more that I don’t always get to them, or when I do, I’m not as able to settle into them because my mind is still kind of busy.\n\nLike, if you asked me, “Do you still enjoy the things you used to enjoy?” I’d say yes, in theory. I haven’t had this feeling of, “Nothing matters” or “I don’t care about anything anymore.” It’s more that I’m distracted, and it takes me longer to relax enough to enjoy something. I’ve always been the one who has the materials ready, answers the email, turns things in when they’re due, keeps track of the moving pieces.\n\nLately I’ve had colleagues remind me about things I used to remember on my own, and even when they’re kind about it, I feel embarrassed. I’ll think, “Why didn’t I just do that already?” or “People are going to think I’m dropping the ball.” So yes, there’s definitely guilt there—not constant every second, but it hangs over me when I know I’ve let something slide.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're on edge and can't relax?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Not every single minute, but a lot more than I’m used to. I feel like I’m kind of wound up most of the time, especially since the district put this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on me on top of my regular teaching. I can get through the school day, and I do, but there’s this pressure sitting there that doesn’t really let up.\n\nEven when I’m technically done for the day, I’ll still be thinking about lesson plans I haven’t finished, or emails I should have answered, or some piece of the rollout that I’m supposed to be tracking. And then I have a hard time actually relaxing because it feels like there’s always something I’m behind on. It’s discouraging, honestly, because I’m usually pretty dependable with those things. I used to just handle lesson plans and emails without even thinking that much about it, and now I find myself putting them off because I feel overwhelmed before I even start.\n\nSo yes, I’d say I’m on edge. I’m functioning, but I don’t feel like myself.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. I mean, I haven’t been tracking my weight carefully or anything like that, so I don’t want to say absolutely, but I haven’t noticed my clothes fitting differently or anyone commenting that I look thinner.\n\nMy eating has maybe been a little less organized just because I’m busy and distracted, but in terms of a significant amount of weight loss, no, I don’t think that’s happened.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad most of the day, every day?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say sad most of the day, every day, no. It’s more that I feel discouraged and wound up, if that makes sense. I came in because this really started after the district put the bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on my plate on top of my regular teaching, and since then I just haven’t felt like myself.\n\nI’m still getting through the day. I’m still showing up, teaching, doing what needs to be done in the classroom. I haven’t had this feeling of, “Nothing matters” or “I don’t care about anything anymore.” It’s more that I’m distracted, and it takes me longer to relax enough to enjoy something. Sometimes I’ll start doing something I normally like and then realize I’m thinking about what I should be doing instead.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I’ve lost interest, not in a big or complete way. It’s more that stress is crowding things out.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble falling or staying asleep because you're thinking about work or other things?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to separate that out, honestly. I mean, there are nights where my mind is still going when I get into bed, and work can be part of that, but I don’t know that I’d say it’s often in a clear, consistent way.\n\nSometimes I’ll lie there and think, “Did I respond to that email?” or “What do I need to do tomorrow?” and then I have to kind of remind myself that I’m in bed and there’s nothing I can do about it right then. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I think… yes, but maybe not in the sense of there being absolutely no consequence. It’s more like the consequence isn’t immediate, or it’s not something anyone is standing over me about, and I still can’t quite let it go.

A lot of it started when the district put this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on my plate, on top of my regular teaching. I came in because since then I’ve still been getting through the day — I’m showing up, I’m teaching, I’m functioning — but I feel wound up and discouraged in a way that really isn’t like me.

And what’s strange is that even when there isn’t some hard deadline that minute, I’ll be thinking about pieces of it. Like, “Should I have already responded to that email?” or “Do I need to adjust that lesson plan?” or “Am I going to be behind if I don’t get to it tonight?” Even things I used to just handle without a second thought, like lesson plans and emails, I’ve started putting off, and then the fact that I’m putting them off makes me worry more.

So yes, I guess I do worry even when there’s not an obvious deadline right then. It still feels connected to the workload, though, not completely random.

I’m not walking around feeling blank or like I’m watching my life from the outside.\n\nThere are moments during the school day where I’m very much on autopilot, but that’s also kind of part of teaching sometimes, you know? I still can enjoy things when I’m actually doing them, if that makes sense. Like if I’m with my family, or if I’m watching something I like, or doing something quiet at home, I can still feel that it’s pleasant.\n\nI think what’s changed more is that it takes a little more effort to get myself into those things, because my mind is kind of pulled back toward school and what I still need to deal with. Not every single minute, but a lot more than I’m used to. I can get through the school day, and I do, but there’s this pressure sitting there that doesn’t really let up.\n\nEven when I’m technically done for the day, I’ll still be thinking about lesson plans I haven’t finished, or emails I should have answered, or some piece of the rollout that I’m supposed to be tracking. I’m functioning, but I don’t feel like myself.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. It’s more that I feel discouraged and wound up, if that makes sense. I’m still showing up, teaching, doing what needs to be done in the classroom. But there’s this heaviness around the work now, especially with things I used to handle automatically—lesson plans, emails, keeping track of what needs to go where. I’ve started putting some of that off, which is very unlike me, and then I feel worse because I know I’m not operating the way I normally do.\n\nSo, not sad all day every day exactly. More stressed, discouraged, and kind of worn down by it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that, because I don’t think it’s like I’ve stopped liking things, exactly. I still know what I enjoy, if that makes sense. It’s more that I don’t always get to them, or when I do, I’m not as able to settle into them because my mind is still kind of busy.\n\nLike, if you asked me, “Do you still enjoy the things you used to enjoy?” I’d say yes, in theory. I haven’t had this feeling of, “Nothing matters” or “I don’t care about anything anymore.” It’s more that I’m distracted, and it takes me longer to relax enough to enjoy something. Sometimes I’ll start doing something I normally like and then realize I’m thinking about what I should be doing instead.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I’ve lost interest, not in a big or complete way. It’s more that stress is crowding things out.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble falling or staying asleep because you're thinking about work or other things?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to separate that out, honestly. I mean, there are nights where my mind is still going when I get into bed, and work can be part of that, but I don’t know that I’d say it’s often in a clear, consistent way.\n\nSometimes I’ll lie there and think, “Did I respond to that email?” or “What do I need to do tomorrow?” and then I have to kind of remind myself that I’m in bed and there’s nothing I can do about it right then. But I haven’t really been keeping track of how many nights that happens, or whether it’s the main reason I’m not sleeping well.\n\nSo… maybe occasionally, but I’m not sure I’d describe it as a major or constant problem. It’s more that my brain takes a while to quiet down sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about things even when there's no apparent reason to worry?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I would put it that way, exactly. I mean, I do worry, but usually I can connect it to something, even if the thing is maybe bigger in my head than it needs to be at the moment.\n\nLike, I’m not just sitting there out of nowhere thinking something terrible is going to happen for no reason. It’s more that if there’s something unresolved, or something I’m not sure I handled correctly, my mind can circle back to it. But there’s usually a reason I can point to, even if I know I may be dwelling on it too much.\n\nSo… I don’t think I’m worrying for no apparent reason, no. It feels more tied to specific things.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about things even when there's no apparent deadline or consequence?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, but maybe not in the sense of there being absolutely no consequence. It’s more like the consequence isn’t immediate, or it’s not something anyone is standing over me about, and I still can’t quite let it go.\n\nA lot of it started when the district put this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on my plate, on top of my regular teaching. I came in because since then I’ve still been getting through the day — I’m showing up, I’m teaching, I’m functioning — but I feel wound up and discouraged in a way that really isn’t like me.\n\nAnd what’s strange is that even when there isn’t some hard deadline that minute, I’ll be thinking about pieces of it. It still feels connected to the workload, though, not completely random.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you had any thoughts that you'd be better off dead or that you wish you wouldn't wake up?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 56 / elementary_or_middle_school_teacher / graduate\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No… no, I haven’t had thoughts like that. I mean, I’ve definitely had moments where I feel overwhelmed or just exhausted by everything, and I’ll think, “I can’t believe I have to deal with all of this again tomorrow,” but that’s not the same as not wanting to wake up.

I don’t wish I were dead. I haven’t thought about hurting myself or anything like that. It’s more that I want things to ease up, or I want to feel like myself again. But no, I haven’t had those kinds of thoughts.

---------- turn 18 | doctor:inference | doctor.build_inference_prompt() | model=meta-llama/Llama-3.3-70B-Instruct ----------

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.0, F32.1, F43.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because I just haven’t been feeling like myself lately. I’m a teacher, and the district put this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on my plate on top of my regular teaching responsibilities, and I know that probably sounds like just another school initiative, because there are always initiatives, but this one has really gotten under my skin.\n\nI’m still showing up, I’m still getting through the school day, I’m not falling apart or anything, but I feel wound up almost all the time and discouraged in a way that’s not typical for me. And I’ve noticed I’m putting off lesson plans and emails—things I used to just sit down and handle without even thinking much about it. Now I’ll stare at them, or tell myself I’ll do them later, and then they just sit there. That’s really not how I usually operate, so I thought maybe I should talk to someone before it gets worse.\n\nDoctor: Are you still enjoying activities or hobbies that you typically found pleasurable before feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that, honestly. I mean, I haven’t really sat down and taken inventory of that. I still do some of the things I normally do, or at least I intend to, but I don’t know if I’ve noticed a clear “I don’t enjoy anything anymore” kind of thing.\n\nIt’s more like everything has this layer of distraction on top of it, if that makes sense. Like if I’m doing something I usually like, part of my brain is still circling around school stuff or thinking about what I should be doing instead. So I wouldn’t say I can’t enjoy things, but I’m not sure I’m enjoying them in the same relaxed way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad most of the time since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say sad most of the time, exactly. Discouraged, yes. Wound up, definitely. It’s more like since the district added this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on top of my regular teaching, I’ve felt kind of unlike myself.\n\nI’m still getting through the day and doing my job, but there’s this constant pressure in the background, and I notice myself putting off lesson plans and emails that I used to just handle without making a whole production out of it. So it’s not that I’m crying all day or feeling deeply sad every minute. It’s more that I feel tense and worn down and kind of disheartened in a way that doesn’t feel normal for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about things that are coming up, like the school initiative or your teaching responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. I mean, I don’t know if I’d call it worrying in the sense of imagining disasters, exactly, but my mind keeps going back to it. Ever since the district put this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on me in addition to my regular teaching, it’s like there’s this constant hum in the background.\n\nI’ll be getting through the school day, and I am getting through it, but I feel wound up and discouraged in a way that really doesn’t feel like me. And then when I think about what’s coming up—lesson plans, emails, whatever needs to be done for this initiative—I sort of freeze or avoid it. Those are things I used to just sit down and handle without much thought, and now I find myself putting them off, which then gives me more to think about. So yes, it’s been taking up a lot more space in my head than I’d like.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious most of the time since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Most of the time might be a little strong, but… yes, I’ve felt much more wound up than usual. It’s not like I’m panicking all day or anything like that. I still show up, I still teach, I still do what I’m supposed to do during the day.\n\nBut ever since the district added this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on top of my regular teaching, I feel like I’m carrying this pressure around with me. I’m discouraged in a way that doesn’t really feel like me, and I notice I’m putting off things like lesson plans and emails—things I used to just sit down and do without turning them into this whole ordeal in my head.\n\nSo I don’t know if I’d say “anxious most of the time” exactly, but I do feel tense and preoccupied with it a lot of the time.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in your weight since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Not that I’ve really noticed, no. I mean, my clothes seem to fit about the same, and I haven’t been weighing myself regularly enough to say, “Oh, it’s exactly the same” or anything like that.\n\nI suppose with everything going on I haven’t been paying close attention to it, but nothing has stood out to me as a real weight change.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding certain situations or places because of how you've been feeling?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’m avoiding places, exactly. I still go to school, I still stand in front of the class, I still get through the teaching day. It’s more that I’ve started avoiding certain tasks connected to all of this, if that makes sense.\n\nSince the district put this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on my plate on top of my regular teaching, I notice I’ll put off sitting down with lesson plans or responding to emails related to it. Those are things I used to just do—maybe not joyfully, but without this big internal hurdle. Now I’ll find myself finding other things to do first, or telling myself I’ll get to it later, and then it’s hanging over me.\n\nSo no, not avoiding a building or a particular room or anything like that. It’s more avoiding the work that makes me feel wound up and discouraged.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt disconnected from your emotions or like you're just going through the motions of daily life without feeling much of anything?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in the sense of feeling numb or detached from myself. I still feel things pretty clearly—maybe too clearly sometimes, honestly. I know when I’m frustrated, or discouraged, or tense. I’m not walking around feeling blank or like I’m watching my life from the outside.\n\nThere are moments during the school day where I’m very much on autopilot, but that’s also kind of part of teaching sometimes, you know? You move from one thing to the next because the day demands it. But I wouldn’t say I feel emotionally disconnected. I’m present, I’m just… tired of having this particular pressure sitting there in the background.\n\nDoctor: Are there things you used to enjoy that you no longer find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I no longer enjoy things, exactly. I still can enjoy things when I’m actually doing them, if that makes sense. Like if I’m with my family, or if I’m watching something I like, or doing something quiet at home, I can still feel that it’s pleasant.\n\nI think what’s changed more is that it takes a little more effort to get myself into those things, because my mind is kind of pulled back toward school and what I still need to deal with. So sometimes something that should feel relaxing doesn’t feel as relaxing as it normally would, but I wouldn’t say the pleasure is gone.\n\nIt’s more like the stress is crowding the edges of things I normally enjoy, not that I can’t enjoy them at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things that you're not getting done or the way you're handling your responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do feel guilty about it, probably more than I’d like to admit. I’m still showing up, and I’m still teaching, and the kids are being taught—I don’t want to make it sound like I’ve stopped doing my job. The basic responsibilities are getting done.\n\nBut there are things around the planning and the rollout work that I’ve either missed the deadline for or pushed back longer than I should have. And that bothers me because I’m not usually that person. I’ve always been the one who has the materials ready, answers the email, turns things in when they’re due, keeps track of the moving pieces.\n\nLately I’ve had colleagues remind me about things I used to remember on my own, and even when they’re kind about it, I feel embarrassed. I’ll think, “Why didn’t I just do that already?” or “People are going to think I’m dropping the ball.” So yes, there’s definitely guilt there—not constant every second, but it hangs over me when I know I’ve let something slide.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're on edge and can't relax?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Not every single minute, but a lot more than I’m used to. I feel like I’m kind of wound up most of the time, especially since the district put this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on me on top of my regular teaching. I can get through the school day, and I do, but there’s this pressure sitting there that doesn’t really let up.\n\nEven when I’m technically done for the day, I’ll still be thinking about lesson plans I haven’t finished, or emails I should have answered, or some piece of the rollout that I’m supposed to be tracking. And then I have a hard time actually relaxing because it feels like there’s always something I’m behind on. It’s discouraging, honestly, because I’m usually pretty dependable with those things. I used to just handle lesson plans and emails without even thinking that much about it, and now I find myself putting them off because I feel overwhelmed before I even start.\n\nSo yes, I’d say I’m on edge. I’m functioning, but I don’t feel like myself.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. I mean, I haven’t been tracking my weight carefully or anything like that, so I don’t want to say absolutely, but I haven’t noticed my clothes fitting differently or anyone commenting that I look thinner.\n\nMy eating has maybe been a little less organized just because I’m busy and distracted, but in terms of a significant amount of weight loss, no, I don’t think that’s happened.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad most of the day, every day?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say sad most of the day, every day, no. It’s more that I feel discouraged and wound up, if that makes sense. I came in because this really started after the district put the bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on my plate on top of my regular teaching, and since then I just haven’t felt like myself.\n\nI’m still getting through the day. I’m still showing up, teaching, doing what needs to be done in the classroom. But there’s this heaviness around the work now, especially with things I used to handle automatically—lesson plans, emails, keeping track of what needs to go where. I’ve started putting some of that off, which is very unlike me, and then I feel worse because I know I’m not operating the way I normally do.\n\nSo, not sad all day every day exactly. More stressed, discouraged, and kind of worn down by it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that, because I don’t think it’s like I’ve stopped liking things, exactly. I still know what I enjoy, if that makes sense. It’s more that I don’t always get to them, or when I do, I’m not as able to settle into them because my mind is still kind of busy.\n\nLike, if you asked me, “Do you still enjoy the things you used to enjoy?” I’d say yes, in theory. I haven’t had this feeling of, “Nothing matters” or “I don’t care about anything anymore.” It’s more that I’m distracted, and it takes me longer to relax enough to enjoy something. Sometimes I’ll start doing something I normally like and then realize I’m thinking about what I should be doing instead.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I’ve lost interest, not in a big or complete way. It’s more that stress is crowding things out.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble falling or staying asleep because you're thinking about work or other things?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to separate that out, honestly. I mean, there are nights where my mind is still going when I get into bed, and work can be part of that, but I don’t know that I’d say it’s often in a clear, consistent way.\n\nSometimes I’ll lie there and think, “Did I respond to that email?” or “What do I need to do tomorrow?” and then I have to kind of remind myself that I’m in bed and there’s nothing I can do about it right then. But I haven’t really been keeping track of how many nights that happens, or whether it’s the main reason I’m not sleeping well.\n\nSo… maybe occasionally, but I’m not sure I’d describe it as a major or constant problem. It’s more that my brain takes a while to quiet down sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about things even when there's no apparent reason to worry?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I would put it that way, exactly. I mean, I do worry, but usually I can connect it to something, even if the thing is maybe bigger in my head than it needs to be at the moment.\n\nLike, I’m not just sitting there out of nowhere thinking something terrible is going to happen for no reason. It’s more that if there’s something unresolved, or something I’m not sure I handled correctly, my mind can circle back to it. But there’s usually a reason I can point to, even if I know I may be dwelling on it too much.\n\nSo… I don’t think I’m worrying for no apparent reason, no. It feels more tied to specific things.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about things even when there's no apparent deadline or consequence?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, but maybe not in the sense of there being absolutely no consequence. It’s more like the consequence isn’t immediate, or it’s not something anyone is standing over me about, and I still can’t quite let it go.\n\nA lot of it started when the district put this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on my plate, on top of my regular teaching. I came in because since then I’ve still been getting through the day — I’m showing up, I’m teaching, I’m functioning — but I feel wound up and discouraged in a way that really isn’t like me.\n\nAnd what’s strange is that even when there isn’t some hard deadline that minute, I’ll be thinking about pieces of it. Like, “Should I have already responded to that email?” or “Do I need to adjust that lesson plan?” or “Am I going to be behind if I don’t get to it tonight?” Even things I used to just handle without a second thought, like lesson plans and emails, I’ve started putting off, and then the fact that I’m putting them off makes me worry more.\n\nSo yes, I guess I do worry even when there’s not an obvious deadline right then. It still feels connected to the workload, though, not completely random.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts that you'd be better off dead or that you wish you wouldn't wake up?\n\nPatient: No… no, I haven’t had thoughts like that. I mean, I’ve definitely had moments where I feel overwhelmed or just exhausted by everything, and I’ll think, “I can’t believe I have to deal with all of this again tomorrow,” but that’s not the same as not wanting to wake up.\n\nI don’t wish I were dead. I haven’t thought about hurting myself or anything like that. It’s more that I want things to ease up, or I want to feel like myself again. But no, I haven’t had those kinds of thoughts.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [doctor] ==========
{"candidates": ["F32.0", "F32.1", "F43.0"], "note": "", "is_final": false}

I came in because this really started after the district put the bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on my plate on top of my regular teaching, and since then I just haven’t felt like myself.\n\nI’m still getting through the day. I’m still showing up, teaching, doing what needs to be done in the classroom. But there’s this heaviness around the work now, especially with things I used to handle automatically—lesson plans, emails, keeping track of what needs to go where. I’ve started putting some of that off, which is very unlike me, and then I feel worse because I know I’m not operating the way I normally do.\n\nSo, not sad all day every day exactly. More stressed, discouraged, and kind of worn down by it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that, because I don’t think it’s like I’ve stopped liking things, exactly. I still know what I enjoy, if that makes sense. It’s more that I don’t always get to them, or when I do, I’m not as able to settle into them because my mind is still kind of busy.\n\nLike, if you asked me, “Do you still enjoy the things you used to enjoy?” I’d say yes, in theory. I haven’t had this feeling of, “Nothing matters” or “I don’t care about anything anymore.” It’s more that I’m distracted, and it takes me longer to relax enough to enjoy something. Sometimes I’ll start doing something I normally like and then realize I’m thinking about what I should be doing instead.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I’ve lost interest, not in a big or complete way. It’s more that stress is crowding things out.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble falling or staying asleep because you're thinking about work or other things?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to separate that out, honestly. It’s more that if there’s something unresolved, or something I’m not sure I handled correctly, my mind can circle back to it. But there’s usually a reason I can point to, even if I know I may be dwelling on it too much.\n\nSo… I don’t think I’m worrying for no apparent reason, no. It feels more tied to specific things.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about things even when there's no apparent deadline or consequence?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, but maybe not in the sense of there being absolutely no consequence. It’s more like the consequence isn’t immediate, or it’s not something anyone is standing over me about, and I still can’t quite let it go.\n\nA lot of it started when the district put this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on my plate, on top of my regular teaching. I came in because since then I’ve still been getting through the day — I’m showing up, I’m teaching, I’m functioning — but I feel wound up and discouraged in a way that really isn’t like me.\n\nAnd what’s strange is that even when there isn’t some hard deadline that minute, I’ll be thinking about pieces of it. Yes… yes, definitely more than a few days. It’s been much longer than that.

I’d say this has been going on for about five months now, since the district assigned me that rollout role midyear. Sometimes I’ll start doing something I normally like and then realize I’m thinking about what I should be doing instead.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I’ve lost interest, not in a big or complete way. It’s more that stress is crowding things out.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble falling or staying asleep because you're thinking about work or other things?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to separate that out, honestly. I mean, there are nights where my mind is still going when I get into bed, and work can be part of that, but I don’t know that I’d say it’s often in a clear, consistent way.\n\nSometimes I’ll lie there and think, “Did I respond to that email?” or “What do I need to do tomorrow?” and then I have to kind of remind myself that I’m in bed and there’s nothing I can do about it right then. But I haven’t really been keeping track of how many nights that happens, or whether it’s the main reason I’m not sleeping well.\n\nSo… maybe occasionally, but I’m not sure I’d describe it as a major or constant problem. It’s more that my brain takes a while to quiet down sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about things even when there's no apparent reason to worry?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I would put it that way, exactly. I mean, I do worry, but usually I can connect it to something, even if the thing is maybe bigger in my head than it needs to be at the moment.\n\nLike, I’m not just sitting there out of nowhere thinking something terrible is going to happen for no reason. It’s more that if there’s something unresolved, or something I’m not sure I handled correctly, my mind can circle back to it. But there’s usually a reason I can point to, even if I know I may be dwelling on it too much.\n\nSo… I don’t think I’m worrying for no apparent reason, no. It feels more tied to specific things.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about things even when there's no apparent deadline or consequence?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, but maybe not in the sense of there being absolutely no consequence. It’s more like the consequence isn’t immediate, or it’s not something anyone is standing over me about, and I still can’t quite let it go.\n\nA lot of it started when the district put this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on my plate, on top of my regular teaching. I came in because since then I’ve still been getting through the day — I’m showing up, I’m teaching, I’m functioning — but I feel wound up and discouraged in a way that really isn’t like me.\n\nAnd what’s strange is that even when there isn’t some hard deadline that minute, I’ll be thinking about pieces of it. Like, “Should I have already responded to that email?” or “Do I need to adjust that lesson plan?” or “Am I going to be behind if I don’t get to it tonight?” Even things I used to just handle without a second thought, like lesson plans and emails, I’ve started putting off, and then the fact that I’m putting them off makes me worry more.\n\nSo yes, I guess I do worry even when there’s not an obvious deadline right then. It still feels connected to the workload, though, not completely random.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts that you'd be better off dead or that you wish you wouldn't wake up?\n\nPatient: No… no, I haven’t had thoughts like that. I mean, I’ve definitely had moments where I feel overwhelmed or just exhausted by everything, and I’ll think, “I can’t believe I have to deal with all of this again tomorrow,” but that’s not the same as not wanting to wake up.\n\nI don’t wish I were dead. I haven’t thought about hurting myself or anything like that. It’s more that I want things to ease up, or I want to feel like myself again. But no, I haven’t had those kinds of thoughts.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about things for more than a few days at a time?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, definitely more than a few days. At first I kept thinking, “Okay, this is just a busy week,” or “Once I get through this next piece, I’ll settle down.” But then another week would go by, and then another, and I’d still be carrying it around in my head.\n\nSo it hasn’t been just a short stretch of worry. It’s been kind of a steady thing since that assignment was added. Some days are lighter than others, but it’s been there for months.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.
